System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

SCENARIO UNDER REVIEW:

NETGEAR enters into a distribution agreement with Bay Networks, including a provision
prohibiting the total value of returned products from exceeding a specific percentage
of the net shipments invoiced during the immediately preceding quarter (evidence_6).
In Q4, NETGEAR experiences a surge in product returns due to a defect in a newly
launched router. To manage the situation, NETGEAR instructs Bay Networks to temporarily
halt all returns and processes replacement orders to offset returns, as allowed
under the warranty provision. However, the defect is broader than initially anticipated,
and NETGEAR decides to process warranty replacements for an additional product line
without formally documenting this under warranty claims. This action creates a mismatch:
while NETGEAR calculates returns within the permissible threshold by counting the
undocumented replacements as warranty adjustments, Bay Networks’ official records
still classify these as returns. Concurrently, NETGEAR communicates the decision
using an internal collaboration tool, bypassing the stipulated email channel (evidence_2).
Bay Networks flags the issue, asserting that the undocumented replacements cannot
be reclassified without formal acknowledgment under the warranty provision. Additionally,
Bay Networks highlights evidence_8, which limits liability for damages except under
specific sections. This creates ambiguity as to whether NETGEAR’s reclassification
could trigger liability under those sections. Despite NETGEAR’s insistence that
the total returns remain within the allowable threshold, Bay Networks argues that
the reclassification creates a cascading dependency failure, as the undocumented
replacements undercut the transparency required by the governing clauses.'
</document>

APPLICATION PACKET ANALYSIS:

The agreement text includes governing clauses. Evidence_2 appears in the text and establishes the entire agreement framework between Distributor and NETGEAR for product distribution, with California law as the governing jurisdiction. Evidence_6 contains a return value threshold with redacted percentages and time periods. Evidence_8 includes liability limitations for distributor damages tied to specific sections. The scenario describes a situation where NETGEAR experiences returns surge due to a router defect and issues temporary halt instructions, though the text appears to be cut off mid-sentence.

Continuing from the cut-off text: "without formally documenting this under warranty claims." The core issue involves NETGEAR classifying undocumented replacements as warranty adjustments while Bay Networks officially records them as returns, creating a mismatch. Additionally, NETGEAR communicates decisions through internal collaboration tools rather than the required email channel per evidence_2. The liability limitation in evidence_8 further complicates whether reclassification triggers liability under specific sections.

The scenario presents an actual business case with potential rule violations. Looking at evidence_2, the communication method is at odds with what the agreement requires—the packet explicitly uses "internal collaboration tool" instead of the stipulated email channel. The core question is whether the text reflects a violation of evidence_2's communication protocols.

For evidence_6, the agreement specifies return value caps relative to net shipments within defined periods, though the packet leaves the specific percentage as a redacted placeholder. This suggests the threshold exists in the underlying agreement but isn't fully visible in the current text.

Moving to evidence_8, I notice a discrepancy—the agreement text contains "IN NO EVENT WELL" which appears to be a typo for "SHALL." The liability cap provision references damages from specific sections and limits distributor liability to an amount owed to NETGEAR under the agreement, with another redacted section referencing NETGEAR's or Bay Networks' total liability. The text appears to be cut off mid-provision.

The packet text for evidence_8 substantially mirrors the rule text with only minor variations, primarily the typo difference between "WELL" and "SHALL" in the first clause, suggesting these are intended to represent the same contractual provision.

For evidence_2, the scenario explicitly states NETGEAR used an internal collaboration tool instead of the stipulated email channel—this represents a clear violation of the communication method specified in the agreement.

Evidence_6 presents a more complex issue. The scenario describes NETGEAR calculating returns within the permissible threshold by treating undocumented replacements as warranty adjustments, yet Bay Networks' records classify the same items as returns. This creates a fundamental accounting discrepancy: the two parties have different totals for the same transactions, suggesting at least one party has violated the transparency or documentation requirements of the agreement.

For evidence_8, the scenario references the liability limitation clause and Bay Networks' assertion that undocumented reclassification could expose NETGEAR to liability under specific sections. The issue appears to hinge on whether the reclassification itself constitutes a breach that would trigger the damages exception outlined in the agreement.

Since the scenario frames this as a dispute rather than a confirmed violation—Bay Networks raises the concern but NETGEAR disputes it—the evidence doesn't establish that a breach has actually occurred. The liability clause remains a potential mechanism, but without a clear determination that reclassification was improper, the status for this rule remains uncertain. The canonical fields are all null since the application packet lacks any structured data fields—the content is entirely narrative description. The disposition should be "needs_correction" because evidence_2 was violated through NETGEAR's use of an internal collaboration tool instead of the required email channel. While evidence_6 presents a counting mismatch and evidence_8 references the liability limitation clause, these don't constitute outright violations but rather factual disputes about whether the breach occurred.

platform, StreamSphere, which did not exist at the time the agreement was signed
but was explicitly covered under the clause allowing distribution on ''distribution
outlets that exist today or that is created or developed in the future.'' However,
ConvergTV did not prominently announce the availability of the Program on StreamSphere
to the Producer, nor did it feature the Program on the homepage of StreamSphere,
instead listing it in a less-trafficked ''Archived Programs'' section. Additionally,
an informal email from ConvergTV’s content manager to the Producer mentioned the
listing but was phrased ambiguously, stating, ''The Program is live somewhere on
StreamSphere. Let us know if you have questions.'' Producer later criticized ConvergTV,
claiming they failed to provide ''meaningful'' distribution of the Program, as the
placement in the ''Archived Programs'' section made it unlikely to garner significant
viewership. Producer further argued that the informal email communication about
the distribution status was insufficient notice under the terms of the contract.
Despite these concerns, ConvergTV maintained that their actions were compliant with
the explicit terms of the agreement, as they had distributed the Program within
the Licensed Territory and during the License Term, on an outlet that was covered
by the contract’s future distribution clauses.'

  • severity: major
  • category: contract_clause
  • description: This describes the licensing grant from Producer to ConvergTV for distribution rights, including episodes/series, license term (2 years), unlimited runs, commencing November 15, 2012, rights to distribute on ConvergTV channels and distribution outlets (current and future), including affiliate channels.

The application packet contains:

  1. The agreement text that matches evidence_5 exactly
  2. A scenario describing a dispute about whether ConvergTV's distribution was "meaningful"

Now I need to verify the rule against the packet.

The rule (evidence_5) describes what the contract grants - it establishes the licensing terms. The question is: what is being verified?

Looking at the description, evidence_5 appears to be a rule that describes the contract clause itself - it's a descriptive rule about what the license grants.

The scenario mentions:

  • ConvergTV distributed the Program on StreamSphere (a new platform)
  • StreamSphere was covered under the "distribution outlets that exist today or that is created or developed in the future" clause
  • Producer claims distribution wasn't "meaningful"
  • ConvergTV maintains their actions were compliant

The evidence_5 rule seems to be defining the scope of the license grant. The question is whether this is being violated or not.

Looking at the packet:

  • The Program was distributed within the Licensed Territory ✓
  • During the License Term (2 years starting November 15, 2012) ✓
  • On StreamSphere, which is a distribution outlet covered by the contract ✓

The rule (evidence_5) describes the license grant - it establishes what rights were granted. The packet shows that ConvergTV did distribute the Program within those granted rights (territory, term, on covered outlets).

So it appears the rule is satisfied - the license grant described in evidence_5 is present in the agreement, and ConvergTV's distribution activities fall within that grant.

The contract clause in evidence_5 is present and intact, with ConvergTV distributing within the Licensed Territory, during the License Term, on a covered platform. The dispute centers on whether the distribution was "meaningful," but the rule itself only establishes the scope of the license grant—it doesn't impose quality or prominence requirements. The evidence_5 rule is satisfied as written.

and its impact on the contractual relationship but was buried within a broader message
discussing routine operational updates. This included updates on unrelated product
launches, marketing plans, and minor contractual amendments, making the notice difficult
to identify as the formal notification required under the agreement. Separately,
the Licensee continued clinical development of Competitive Products through a joint
venture (JV) arrangement with a Third Party. Licensee argued that this JV activity
was conducted solely ''in connection with'' the Competitor Change of Control, which

rights to Section 15.2.9. However, Bioeq contested that the JV arrangement violated
Section 16.4, which prohibits transfers of rights or obligations to Third Parties
without prior written consent. Licensee justified the JV by asserting that the arrangement
was not a transfer but rather a subcontracting activity permissible under the agreement.
Additionally, Licensee claimed that even if prior consent was required, the Competitor
Change of Control exempted these activities under the governing provisions.'

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

  • id: evidence_6
  • severity: major
  • category: contract_clause
  • description: This is a complex rule that describes:
  1. A license grant requirement for countries requiring specific licenses for joint owners
  2. The joint ownership arrangement
  3. The assignment of joint equal and undivided interest

  • A scenario describing how the license was granted via a shared digital document rather than a formal written agreement

  1. Extract canonical fields (there are none listed to extract from)
  2. Report EVERY rule exactly once with the same rule_id
  3. Determine if the rule is violated, ok, or not_applicable
  4. The disposition depends on whether there are violations

Looking at the scenario: The third-party reviewer flags the method of granting the license as questionable because it wasn't through a traditional signed legal document. Bioeq argues that the clause doesn't specify format requirements.

The key question: Does the rule specify how the license must be granted (format/formal written vs. any method)?

Looking at the rule text:

  • It says "each Party hereby grants to the other Party a perpetual, irrevocable, non-exclusive, worldwide, royalty-free, fully paid-up license"
  • It does NOT specify that the grant must be in a formal written document
  • It does NOT specify the format of the license grant

The scenario presents a situation where:

  • The license was granted (just through digital collaboration document)
  • The license terms match (perpetual, irrevocable, non-exclusive, worldwide, royalty-free, fully paid-up, transferable and sublicensable)
  • The grant happened, just not through a traditional signed document

The rule does NOT require a specific format for the license grant. The rule simply states "hereby grants" without specifying format requirements.

Bioeq's argument has merit since the clause is ambiguous about format—the license was indeed granted with matching terms, just through an unconventional method. The scenario frames this as questionable from a third-party audit perspective, but the rule itself contains no explicit format mandate. Since the rule describes what must occur rather than prescribing how it must be documented, the unconventional digital method appears consistent with the substantive requirements. The rule doesn't explicitly mandate a written format—it just requires that the license be granted. Since the scenario describes the license being granted through a digital document with a timestamp and cloud storage, with explicit acceptance by Licensee, this could satisfy the grant requirement even if it deviates from traditional formalities.

However, the third-party reviewer flagged this as questionable, which creates tension. In a compliance audit context, the question becomes whether this unconventional method constitutes a violation of the rule's requirements.

The disposition hinges on how strictly the rule should be interpreted. If the license was truly granted with matching terms, the substance appears satisfied. But if the rule implicitly requires formal documentation, the absence of a traditional signed document might constitute a violation. Given that the reviewer flagged this as problematic and this is a major severity rule, "needs_correction" seems more appropriate than acceptance, since the method raises legitimate concerns even if the underlying license grant isn't entirely absent. The agreement text contains all three components: the license grant clause, joint ownership language, and the assignment of equal undivided interest. Looking at the verification checklist, each element is present and matches the required structure—the parties grant the license, jointly own the inventions, and assign undivided interests accordingly. The rule is satisfied by the document's content.

The scenario's procedural concern about how the license was granted falls outside the rule's scope, which focuses on what the clause must contain rather than execution mechanics. Since the agreement text includes everything the rule requires, there's no violation to report. The third-party reviewer's objection about execution method is relevant to compliance practices but doesn't indicate the rule itself was breached.

With no violations identified, the overall disposition is "accept." Since no specific fields are mandated for extraction, the "extracted" object remains empty.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

Thinking · скрыто по умолчанию

Let me analyze this carefully according to the ruleset ace-0006 vace1.

The ruleset has 2 rules:

  1. evidence_12 - about the "Evaluation Material" definition and exclusions (non-confidential basis from third party)
  2. evidence_7 - about retention exceptions for archived backups and regulatory requirements

I need to verify each rule against the application packet and scenario.

Rule evidence_12:
This rule describes when information is EXCLUDED from "Evaluation Material" - specifically information that "was or becomes available to you on a non-confidential basis from a person (other than the Company or its Representatives) who, to your knowledge, is not otherwise bound by a confidentiality agreement."

The scenario describes:

  • Company A's IT team discovered a folder with Evaluation Material
  • They believed it fell under the 'non-confidential basis' exception (evidence_12)
  • They moved it to a shared drive for review
  • A junior employee accessed it and used it in a presentation

The question is: Was this material actually excluded from "Evaluation Material" under evidence_12?

Looking at the scenario:

  • Company A argues the material was obtained from a third-party vendor not bound by confidentiality
  • They provided documentation showing the vendor's non-confidential status

However, the issue is:

  1. The exception in evidence_12 requires the information to be "available to you on a non-confidential basis from a person" - this is about information ORIGINALLY obtained from third parties
  2. The material in question appears to be "Evaluation Material" that was part of the confidential exchange between Company A and Company B
  3. The vendor providing information doesn't automatically make ALL related information non-confidential

The scenario states that Company A "believes" the material falls under this exception, but the rule requires that information "was or becomes available to you on a non-confidential basis from a person." The material being discussed appears to be Evaluation Material that was shared under the agreement, not information that was independently obtained from a third party on a non-confidential basis.

Company A misapplied evidence_12 here. The exception doesn't apply to information shared as Evaluation Material under the agreement—it only covers information independently obtained from third parties without confidentiality obligations. Their characterization that the material qualified as "non-confidential basis" information appears incorrect; the material was part of the confidential exchange itself, making their access and sharing a breach.

For evidence_7, I need to examine what the scenario actually establishes about the backup copies and regulatory retention requirements.

The agreement permits two retention scenarios: automated backup archives and copies needed for legal compliance. Company A maintained copies under both provisions—one encrypted archive and one regulatory record held for seven years. However, the material discovered during the IT upgrade doesn't fall into either category. The backup exception only protects archived material; it doesn't authorize indefinite retention or sharing of information discovered outside automated systems. The regulatory exception similarly doesn't apply to newly found material unless there's a separate legal obligation requiring its preservation. Company A's access and transfer of this material to a shared drive exceeded what the agreement allows.

The evidence_12 violation is clear: Company A mischaracterized unrelated Evaluation Material as third-party information, accessing and distributing it in violation of the agreement. This constitutes a material breach warranting rejection.

The rule (evidence_3) describes a complex multi-conditional stock option clause with three scenarios (a), (b), and (c):

  • (a): >= USD30M upfront AND >= USD300M milestone → option for 44,757 Shares (0.25%)
  • (b): = USD24M upfront AND = USD240M milestone → option for 22,378 Shares (0.125%)
  • (c): > USD24M and < USD30M upfront AND > USD240M and < USD300M milestone → option for Shares between 0.125% and 0.25% (Board discretion)

The packet scenario describes:

  • Transaction with upfront payment of USD25,000,000 and downstream milestone payments of USD290,000,000
  • This clearly falls under condition (c): upfront > USD24M and < USD30M, milestone > USD240M and < USD300M
  • Under condition (c), the Board should grant an option for a number of Shares falling between 0.125% and 0.25%
  • Instead, the Board granted 22,378 Shares (0.125%), which is the minimum/lower end
  • This is technically within the range of 0.125% to 0.25%

Now I need to evaluate whether this is a violation:

The rule says condition (c): "an option to acquire such number of Shares (falling between 0.125% and 0.25% of the Company's issued share capital as at the Effective Date as the Board shall determine in its absolute discretion)"

The key question: Does granting 0.125% (22,378 shares) fall within the range of "between 0.125% and 0.25%"?

The phrase "falling between 0.125% and 0.25%" could be interpreted as strictly exclusive of the endpoints, though mathematical conventions vary. Given the rule's intent to create three distinct scenarios with different share allocations, the most reasonable reading is that the boundaries are inclusive—otherwise scenario (c) would have no valid minimum. The Board's decision to grant the exact minimum (22,378 Shares) aligns with this interpretation.

The rule explicitly grants the Board "absolute discretion" to determine the number of Shares within this range, so there's technically no violation in selecting 0.125%. However, the critical issue is that the Board justified its decision by referencing condition (b) instead of relying on its discretionary authority under condition (c), which suggests a fundamental misunderstanding of how it arrived at the 22,378 figure. The rule states the Board has "absolute discretion" to determine the number of shares within the 0.125% to 0.25% range. Granting 22,378 shares (0.125%) falls within this range, so the choice itself doesn't violate the rule. However, the Board justified the grant by citing condition (b), which applies only to different financial thresholds (USD24M upfront and USD240M milestone). The actual transaction in scenario (c) involved USD25M upfront and USD290M in milestones, so referencing condition (b) as the basis for the grant represents a misapplication of the framework rather than a violation of the discretion itself. The key distinction is whether "falling between 0.125% and 0.25%" includes or excludes the endpoints. Linguistically, "between" typically encompasses the boundaries, so 0.125% would be permissible. However, this creates an overlap with condition (b), which grants exactly 0.125% under different financial terms. The better interpretation is that condition (c) requires strictly interior values—greater than 0.125% and less than 0.25%—making the Board's grant of exactly 0.125% non-compliant. This constitutes a violation because the transaction terms matched condition (c)'s financial parameters, yet the Board applied condition (b)'s share allocation instead. the documentation explicitly showed the Board chose 22,378 Shares instead of exercising discretion within the proper range. Even if the minimum technically satisfies "falling between" linguistically, the real problem is the Board justified it under condition (b) when the financial terms clearly triggered condition (c). This creates ambiguity about whether the contract was properly administered. I'm settling on a violation because the Board's stated rationale references the wrong condition entirely, and while 22,378 might satisfy the minimum threshold mathematically, the documentation and reasoning don't align with what condition (c) actually requires. The scenario is clear: Board approved a deal with USD25M upfront and USD290M milestones, triggering condition (c). Yet the Board granted 22,378 shares—the exact amount from condition (b)—rather than exercising discretion to determine a number within the 0.125%-0.25% range. This constitutes a violation: the documented grant explicitly states the lower-bound option amount without showing any Board deliberation about where within the permitted range to set the award, undermining the exercise of discretion that condition (c) requires. The Board's choice of 0.125% for a deal worth USD25M upfront and USD290M milestones, rather than a higher percentage within the range, represents poor governance but not necessarily a contractual violation. The language "falling between 0.125% and 0.25%" permits either endpoint. That said, the Board's stated justification—referencing condition (b)'s USD24M upfront threshold when the actual transaction involved USD25M—suggests they may have misapplied the contractual framework rather than simply exercising discretion. The violation appears to be in the reasoning process rather than the outcome itself. I'll mark this as a violation of evidence_3, documenting the Board's use of the incorrect condition as the basis for their determination. The overlap occurs because 22,378 shares (0.125%) falls at the boundary—it's simultaneously the minimum under condition (c) and the maximum under condition (b).   • id: evidence_10
  • severity: major
  • category: contract_clause
  • description contains two parts separated by "|":
  1.   2. Upon termination/expiration of Franchise, Franchisor has option (not obligation) exercisable for 30 days upon written notice to purchase at fair market value all assets and take assignment of lease

The application packet scenario describes:

  • Franchise expires March 1, 2023, Lease remains active until December 31, 2023

  • Lessee files complaint March 10, 2023 claiming breach

Part 1 (Lessor cooperation for access/removal):

  • The rule says upon expiration/termination, Lessor will cooperate with Franchisor
  • Lessor did grant access on March 2 (after Franchise expired March 1)
  • This appears compliant - Lessor allowed entry to remove signs and make modifications

Part 2 (Franchisor's option to purchase with written notice):

  • Franchisor sent informal email Feb 25 (before expiration) - vague, no price or asset list
  • Franchisor sent formal written notice March 20 (within 30 days after expiration)
  • Lessor's early access grant on March 2 preceded the formal written notice requirement

The critical issue is whether the informal email satisfied the "written notice" requirement. The rule specifies "thirty (30) days upon written notice to you," meaning the notice must be written and exercise the option. The Feb 25 email was informal and vague—it expressed general intent but lacked specifics on price and asset identification, so it likely didn't constitute valid notice under the contract terms. The formal notice on March 20 came after Lessor had already granted access, raising questions about whether Lessor acted prematurely without proper notice in place. The informal email on Feb 25 is written but vague—expressing intent without specifying price or assets. The formal notice arrives March 20, well within the 30-day window. The real question is whether vague informal notice satisfies the requirement, or if the formal notice on March 20 is what actually triggers the timeline. Lessor granted access on March 2, which was after the Franchise Agreement expired but before the formal written notice was sent. Since the rule requires written notice to exercise the option, and the formal notice didn't come until March 20, Lessor appears to have acted prematurely by allowing access before that notice was provided. that requirement, though the vagueness raises questions about whether it adequately specifies the scope of the option being exercised. The rule also requires written notice to trigger the 30-day window, which the March 20 notice satisfies. Since both agreements are active and the Franchise expires first on March 1, the timing aligns with the rule's triggering condition. Lessor's grant of access on March 2 appears to fall within the cooperation obligation described in Part 1 of the evidence, assuming the written notice requirement is met by the March 20 communication. lessor acts to assist franchisor. Part 2 addresses the franchisor's option to purchase assets with written notice—neither the vague email nor formal notice constitutes notice to the lessor, only to the franchisee. The lessor's grant of access on March 2 appears permissible under part 1's cooperation clause, as the franchise agreement had already expired and access was provided to remove identifying materials. The timing and purpose align with the lessor's obligation to assist. The written notice requirement in part 2 doesn't actually trigger any obligation on the lessor's side—it only gives the franchisor the option to purchase. The lessor has no duty arising from that notice. System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

Let me analyze this task carefully.

The ruleset ace-0011 has one rule:

  • id: evidence_2
  • severity: major
  • category: contract_clause
  • description: "To be covered by the Seller for 110% invoice value against All Risks and War Risk."

The application packet is a document with id "agreement" that contains:

  1. A clause that says: "To be covered by the Seller for 110% invoice value against All Risks and War Risk."
  2. A scenario description that explains a situation where the Seller uses two separate policies (All Risks for 110% and War Risk for 100%) but the total still meets the 110% requirement. The communication method was unconventional (comment on cloud storage), but the policies remain valid.

Now I need to verify this rule against the packet.

The rule requires: 110% invoice value coverage against All Risks and War Risk by the Seller.

What does the packet show?

  • The clause in the agreement states: "To be covered by the Seller for 110% invoice value against All Risks and War Risk."
  • The scenario describes that the Seller arranges coverage with two policies: one for All Risks at 110% and one for War Risk at 100%.
  • The total coverage fulfills 110% invoice value.
  • The split policies are not prohibited by contract language.

So the question is: does having All Risks at 110% and War Risk at 100% (total 110%) satisfy the requirement?

The requirement says "110% invoice value against All Risks and War Risk." This could be interpreted as:

  1. Total coverage of 110% that covers both All Risks and War Risk
  2. 110% coverage specifically for each risk type

The scenario says "the total coverage across the two policies fulfills the required 110% invoice value, and the separation of risks into different policies is not prohibited by the contract language."

Given that:

The contract language doesn't mandate 110% for each policy, only that coverage meet the 110% threshold overall. The split arrangement—All Risks at 110% and War Risk at 100%—achieves this total requirement, so the rule is satisfied. The communication method used doesn't affect whether the substantive coverage obligation was met.

Let me analyze this verification task carefully.

The ruleset is "ace-0013 vace1" which contains a single rule: evidence_8.

The rule describes what HealthGate must do upon termination. Let me break down the requirements:

  1. Grant non-exclusive non-transferable license for Proprietary Software
  2. Exercise best endeavors to grant non-exclusive non-transferable license for Third Party Software
  3. Develop a Transition Plan with the Publishers
  4. Provide reasonable training for Publishers' personnel
  5. Liaison period: commences when notice is given, continues for max 3 months after termination
  6. Comply with Publishers' reasonable directions upon termination notice
  7. Provide termination assistance to allow Services to continue and facilitate orderly transfer
  8. Continuing to perform Services for up to 6 months following termination date
  9. Render all assistance, provide all documentation, undertake all actions for orderly assumption
  10. If using Publishers' Equipment, may continue until reasonably transitioned
  11. Liaise with Publishers, making available liaison staff as reasonably required, acting in good faith

Now let me examine the scenario:

  1. HealthGate provides a brief informal email confirming receipt - this is acknowledgment but not the full Transition Plan
  2. Instead of drafting a comprehensive Transition Plan, HealthGate uploads a generic template with placeholders - this is NOT compliant. The rule requires "Developing, together with the Publishers, a plan for the orderly transition of Services"
  3. HealthGate fulfills several obligations including providing licenses - this appears compliant
  4. Training sessions via unrecorded video calls without documentation - the rule says "Providing reasonable training" - training without documentation may or may not be reasonable, but the rule doesn't explicitly require documentation for training
  5. HealthGate continues using Publishers' leased equipment for third parties, claiming it can't be transitioned - the rule says "may continue to use that Equipment for that purpose until such time as HealthGate can reasonably transition to other equipment" - this seems permitted
  1. HealthGate provides all required licenses, performs services for required period, and engages in good faith liaison - this appears compliant

Looking at compliance status, the Transition Plan requirement is violated since a generic template doesn't satisfy the "developing together" obligation. For the training requirement, the rule specifies "reasonable training" but doesn't mandate documentation, so the video-only approach likely meets this standard. The equipment provision appears compliant since the rule explicitly allows continued use until reasonable transition is possible.

Checking against all termination obligations: the proprietary software license was granted, best endeavors for third-party software were exercised, reasonable training was provided despite lacking documentation, the liaison period timing wasn't specified as problematic, reasonable directions were followed, termination assistance was given, and the six-month service performance requirement was satisfied.

The main issue centers on the Transition Plan itself. The rule requires HealthGate to develop this collaboratively with the Publishers as an orderly transition document, but instead they uploaded a generic pre-existing template with placeholders that wasn't tailored to the Publishers' operations. This is a direct violation of the "developing, together with the Publishers" language—the collaboration element is missing. The other requirements appear substantially met across license grants, assistance, liaison, and good faith compliance. obligation to develop a plan together with the Publishers. The generic template approach falls short because it lacks the collaborative development aspect and specific tailoring to the Publishers' operations that the rule requires.

So there's a violation on the Transition Plan requirement. I should also consider whether the training documentation issue rises to a violation—the agreement specifies "reasonable training" generally, without explicit documentation requirements, so the video calls without accompanying materials might not constitute a breach, though it could be questioned. The equipment transition timing also seems compliant with what's allowed under the agreement.

Looking at the rule structure, evidence_8 contains multiple distinct obligations that could function as separate rules, but the system treats it as a single rule. The "Developing, together with the Publishers, a plan for the orderly transition of Services" requirement appears to be violated by the generic template approach. I need to determine if this is a critical violation warranting rejection or a non-critical issue requiring correction.

The violation is specific and clearly documented - the pre-existing template with placeholders lacks the collaborative development and tailored details the rule requires. Given that other obligations are substantially met (services performed, licenses provided, good faith liaison maintained), this seems more like a non-critical compliance gap than a fundamental breach of the termination agreement's core purpose.

Let me analyze the ruleset and application packet carefully.

Rules to verify:

  1. evidence_4: Participant not liable for mistakes/errors in judgment or acts/omissions done in good faith within scope of authority
  2. evidence_5: Joint Venture may acquire insurance for Participants, employees, agents on behalf of the Joint Venture against liability from acting in good faith

I need to:

  1. Extract canonical fields (the task says to fill each, but lists no specific fields - I'll note this as an empty extraction since no canonical fields are specified)
  2. Check each rule for violations
  3. Provide findings with evidence
  4. Determine disposition

Let me analyze each rule:

evidence_4: This clause states that a Participant won't be liable for acts done in good faith within scope of authority.

Looking at the scenario:

  • Mark was a contractor (not explicitly stated as a "Participant" - Participants appear to be Company A, B, C)
  • Mark's actions were risky and unauthorized
  • Mark claimed good faith, but implemented a "risky, unauthorized method"
  • The auditors questioned whether Mark's actions constituted "good faith"

The clause applies to Participants. Mark is described as "a contractor" engaged for the project. The Joint Venture is comprised of Company A, B, and C (the Participants). Mark is not a Participant - he is a contractor.

The question is whether Mark's actions meet the "good faith" standard. The auditors questioned this because:

  • He implemented a risky, unauthorized method
  • This resulted in significant damage

The clause requires: acts done in good faith AND believed to be within scope of authority.

Issues:

  1. Mark may not be covered as he's a contractor, not a Participant
  2. Even if covered, there's a dispute about whether his actions were in "good faith" given the unauthorized risky method

For evidence_4:

  • Status: This clause is about non-liability protection, and the scenario discusses whether Mark's actions meet the "good faith" standard
  • The auditors raised questions about this, suggesting potential violation of the good faith requirement

evidence_5: This clause allows the JV to acquire insurance for Participants, employees, agents against liability.

The scenario involves Jane's decision to use an umbrella policy instead of purchasing specific insurance. Key issues: the umbrella policy excluded independent contractors unless named, which Mark wasn't, and the policy wasn't explicitly acquired "for" Mark in the way the clause contemplates. Jane also didn't follow the proper process—she sent an email to the broker rather than formally acquiring insurance as the clause contemplates.

For the evidence assessment, evidence_4 applies to Participants, but Mark is a contractor. Whether he's covered depends on interpreting "any other person engaged in the business interest of the Joint Venture." The clause's language suggests it could extend beyond just the named Participants. Evidence_5 is relevant because the insurance mechanism here relates directly to the coverage question, even though the specific policy choice didn't protect Mark.

On evidence_4, the core question is whether Mark qualifies as a "Participant" under the agreement. The Joint Venture consists of three companies, and Mark is explicitly described as "a contractor"—these appear to be distinct categories. If Mark isn't a Participant, the clause doesn't apply to him at all. Even if he were covered, the auditors challenged whether his actions met the "good faith" standard given his unauthorized approach and the resulting damage.

For evidence_5, the situation differs because this clause explicitly covers "any Participant, employee, agent or other person engaged in the business interest of the Joint Venture"—language that could encompass contractors. However, the clause grants the JV discretionary authority ("may acquire") rather than an obligation. The real problem is that Jane attempted to rely on an existing umbrella policy rather than actively acquiring new coverage for Mark. The policy also explicitly excludes independent contractors unless they're named insureds, and Mark wasn't named. So while the JV had the option to acquire insurance, it didn't do so in a way that actually protected Mark.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

Let me analyze this verification task carefully.

Ruleset: ace-0018 vace1

Rule: evidence_4

  • Severity: major
  • Category: contract_clause
  • Description: solicit any Customers that have a business presence outside the Territory, except that Distributor shall not conduct solicitation activities in any outside territory where Hydraspin is bound to an exclusive distributor agreement with a third party, provided that Hydraspin has notified Distributor in writing of its arrangements with the other distributor and of the territory which is subject to exclusivity in favor of the other distributor. | Distributor shall be entitled to advertise, promote, market or

Application Packet Analysis:

The packet contains:

  1. An agreement clause text that appears to be identical to the rule description (the first part of the description before the "|")
  2. A scenario describing Company A (distributor) launching a marketing campaign targeting Region Y (outside its territory)
  3. Company Z was in an exclusive territory covered by Company C
  4. Hydraspin sent notification on December 15, 2022 about the exclusive arrangement
  5. The notification was vague - referenced "Region Y" but didn't delineate subregions
  6. The email lacked formal letterhead and wasn't marked as legally binding
  7. Company A hosted a webinar inviting businesses across all subregions of Region Y including Company Z
  8. Company Z expressed interest and contacted Company A
  9. Company C accused Company A of breaching the exclusivity clause

Key Question for evidence_4:
The rule says:

  • Distributor shall NOT solicit customers outside the Territory
  • EXCEPT: Distributor shall not conduct solicitation in territories where Hydraspin has an exclusive distributor agreement with a third party
  • PROVIDED THAT: Hydraspin has notified Distributor in writing of:
  1. Its arrangements with the other distributor
  2. The territory which is subject to exclusivity

Analysis of the scenario:

  1. Was there a solicitation outside the Territory?
  • Company A launched marketing campaign in Region Y (outside designated territory) - YES
  • The webinar included Company Z from the exclusive territory - YES
  1. Was there written notification from Hydraspin?
  • Yes, email on December 15, 2022
  1. Was the notification sufficient (did it specify "the territory which is subject to exclusivity")?
  • The notification was vague - it referenced "Region Y" but did NOT delineate subregions
  • The notification lacked formal letterhead and wasn't marked as legally binding

Violation Assessment:

The rule requires specific territorial delineation in the notification. The email failed to provide the granular geographic boundaries needed—referencing Region Y as a whole without identifying which subregions fell under Company C's exclusivity. This vagueness means the "provided that" condition wasn't truly satisfied. Company A's actions in conducting the webinar across all subregions, including Company Z's territory, constitute a violation because the notification lacked the specificity the rule demands. The vagueness in the notification about which subregions were actually exclusive undermines Hydraspin's claim that they properly delineated the territory. Since the notification requirement is a prerequisite for enforcement, the ambiguous email—lacking formal letterhead and not marking subregions as exclusive—means the condition wasn't properly satisfied. This makes the violation stand.

For disposition: critical violations warrant rejection, while non-critical ones allow for correction. The notification defect is fundamental to the rule's enforceability, so this should be marked as reject.

The canonical fields need to be null since no structured data was provided, and the extracted agreement text itself must be included in the output.

Now I'm identifying the specific findings: the rule requires both written notification and clear delineation of exclusive territories, but the email failed on both counts—it vaguely referenced "Region Y" without specifying the subregions subject to exclusivity, and lacked formal letterhead or legal binding language. This resulted in Company A conducting a webinar that reached Company Z in Company C's exclusive territory. The notification requirement isn't merely procedural—it's the mechanism that protects other distributors' exclusive rights, making it a critical requirement rather than a minor formality.

Let me analyze this verification task carefully.

I have:

  1. One rule: evidence_7 from ruleset ace-0019 vace1
  2. The application packet contains an agreement with the relevant clause
  3. I need to verify if the packet complies with the rule

Rule evidence_7 requires:

  • Contractor shall maintain insurance policies
  • Contractor shall cause subcontractors to maintain insurance policies
  • At Contractor's own expense
  • As long as Agreement is in effect
  • Insurance of the kind and limits as set forth in APPENDIX D
  • Keep in force policies from sections 1 and 3 to Insurance Certificate
  • Valid as long as Contractor's legal liability EXISTS IN CONNECTION WITH OPERATIONS ACCORDING TO THE AGREEMENT

Key aspects to check:

  1. Does Contractor maintain insurance? - YES (stated in both agreement and scenario)
  2. Does Contractor cause subcontractors to maintain insurance? - The scenario says subcontractors had separate policies, but the rule says "shall cause any subcontractors engaged by Contractor to provide services under this Agreement to maintain" - the scenario says "the Contractor ensured that all subcontractors maintained insurance policies that complied with the limits and types set forth in APPENDIX D"
  3. At Contractor's own expense? - The agreement says "at Contractor's own expense" but the scenario mentions "subcontractors were insured through separate policies procured directly by each subcontractor" - this is a potential issue because if subcontractors procured their own insurance, the expense wasn't borne by the Contractor
  4. As long as Agreement is in effect? - YES
  5. Insurance of the kind and limits as set forth in APPENDIX D? - YES, both Contractor's and subcontractors' policies complied
  6. Keep in force policies specified in sections 1 and 3 valid as long as legal liability exists? - The scenario mentions "insurance renewal" so policies were kept in force

The key finding:

  • The agreement requires Contractor to "shall cause any subcontractors engaged by Contractor to provide services under this Agreement to maintain" insurance
  • The scenario indicates each subcontractor independently purchased their own coverage rather than the Contractor arranging it, which conflicts with the requirement that the Contractor ensure subcontractors are covered
  • The evidence_7 clause specifically mandates that Contractor bear all insurance expenses, yet the scenario describes subcontractors procuring separate policies on their own
  • This creates a direct contradiction: the language requires Contractor-caused coverage at Contractor's expense, but the actual practice involved subcontractors independently purchasing their own insurance
  • The contractual language about "causing" subcontractors to maintain coverage suggests the Contractor should arrange or ensure this protection, not leave it to individual subcontractors to procure separately
  • If subcontractors are obtaining their own policies, the Contractor isn't bearing that expense as the clause requires
  • The scenario does confirm all subcontractors maintained compliant policies that met APPENDIX D standards and remained valid throughout the period

So the practical outcome aligns with the contract's intent—the subcontractors are covered and the coverage meets requirements—but the execution differs from the strict reading of how the Contractor should have facilitated that coverage. "procured directly by each subcontractor" - this suggests the subcontractors paid for their own insurance, which conflicts with the rule requiring the Contractor to bear all insurance expenses. The clause specifies Contractor shall "cause any subcontractors...to maintain" insurance, but the scenario shows subcontractors independently purchasing coverage. While the end result satisfies the compliance requirement—policies exist and meet standards—the payment structure violates the rule's language about Contractor bearing the expense. The subcontractor coverage requirement in evidence_7 appears to have been met—subcontractors maintained compliant policies. However, a violation exists regarding who bears the insurance costs: the scenario explicitly states subcontractors procured their own policies, whereas evidence_7 requires all insurance expenses to be Contractor's responsibility. The renewal notification issue represents a procedural or professional conduct concern rather than a substantive compliance failure with the contractual rule itself.

Looking at the contract language, it requires subcontractors to maintain insurance as specified in APPENDIX D, and the scenario confirms they did maintain such coverage that met the agreement's limits and types. The subcontractor requirement appears satisfied on this basis.

The critical question is whether "at Contractor's own expense" applies to the subcontractor policies themselves. The scenario states subcontractors procured their own insurance directly, which could suggest a violation if that phrase is meant to cover all insurance costs. However, there's another interpretation: perhaps the rule is specifically about the Contractor's primary policies being at Contractor's expense, while subcontractor coverage follows a different mechanism. Since the Contractor's own policies were maintained at Contractor's expense and subcontractors had valid coverage meeting the standards, this could represent a procedural deviation rather than a substantive violation of the substantive coverage requirements.

The language in evidence_7 is unambiguous—the Contractor must cause subcontractors to maintain insurance, and all expenses fall on the Contractor. The subcontractor policies are explicitly included in the scope of "such insurance." The scenario's indication that subcontractors procured separate policies directly suggests they bore those costs themselves, which would constitute a violation of the expense requirement.

The scenario does confirm compliance with the substantive insurance mandate: subcontractors maintained valid policies covering the required operations, and all documentation remained current throughout the period. The renewal notification issue appears to be a procedural concern rather than a substantive compliance failure.

The core finding is that subcontractors obtained their own separate coverage rather than being included under the Contractor's policy as the contract language contemplates. This represents a deviation from the agreement's requirements, even though the outcome—adequate coverage existed—meets the practical intent. The renewal notification issue is a separate matter—it's about the formality of communication rather than actual insurance compliance. Since the policies were genuinely renewed and valid, and the rule addresses substantive insurance requirements, not notification procedures, that aspect doesn't constitute a violation of evidence_7. The substantive finding is the segregation of subcontractor insurance, which I should classify as a non-critical violation since all subcontractors still maintained compliant policies meeting APPENDIX D standards.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

  1. evidence_10 (major, contract_clause):
  • Requires for Confidential Information disclosure to Representatives:
  • Disclosure only to directors, officers, employees (Representatives)
  • Only to the extent reasonably necessary for internal use
  • Only after informing each Representative of the restrictions and that they must comply
  • Requires for CEII disclosure:
  • Only to Representatives who have properly executed individual non-disclosure or confidentiality agreements in the course of their employment specifically pertaining to confidential information and CEII
  1. evidence_7 (major, contract_clause):
  • For Confidential Information: Same as evidence_10's first part (disclosure to Representatives)
  • Plus: (ii) to any other person or entity only with Disclosing Party's prior written consent
  • Keep Confidential Information strictly confidential
  • Shall not disclose Confidential Information to any third party without prior written consent
  • Shall not disclose CEII to any third party without prior written consent

  • Is a company ✓
  • Shares documentation with its engineering team (who are employees/Representatives) ✓
  • Each team member had signed individual NDAs covering CEII and confidential information ✓
  • Project manager uploaded to Google Drive and shared via Slack
  • Team members were informed of confidentiality restrictions via Slack message

evidence_10 analysis:

  • ✓ Disclosure to Representatives (engineering team = employees)
  • ✓ Reasonably necessary for internal use (internal analysis)
  • ✓ Informed Representatives of restrictions (Slack message note)

For CEII:

  • ✓ Only to Representatives (engineering team)
  • ✓ Each team member had signed individual NDAs covering CEII

The question is: does using Google Drive + Slack violate evidence_10?

The rule focuses on who receives the information and whether they were informed—not the delivery mechanism itself. Since the distribution stayed within the organization, went only to authorized Representatives, and included notification of the confidentiality obligations, evidence_10 is satisfied. The choice of Google Drive and Slack doesn't appear to breach the rule's explicit requirements, which center on recipient restrictions and informed consent rather than mandating specific technical channels.

For evidence_7, the requirements follow the same pattern: disclosure limited to Representatives, restricted to internal use only, and prior notification of restrictions. Additionally, any other disclosure requires written consent, and neither Confidential Information nor CEII can be shared with third parties without authorization. The facts show all three conditions are met—no unauthorized external sharing occurred, the information remained internal, and all recipients were notified. This rule also passes.

Since neither rule contains major violations, the overall disposition is "accept". The canonical fields are all null as requested.

],

}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.

  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".

SCENARIO UNDER REVIEW:

Tripath and Distributor are nearing the end of their agreement, and Distributor
chooses to terminate the agreement under Subsection 18.3. As per the governing clauses,
Distributor is obligated to resell its inventory to Tripath, provided the inventory
is undamaged and in good condition. Tripath performs an on-site inspection and determines
that 85% of the inventory qualifies for purchase under these conditions. However,
Tripath also notes that a subset of inventory includes discontinued Products, which
are expressly excluded from the obligation. Tripath communicates this determination
to Distributor via an unconventional method—a comment in a collaborative document
shared on a cloud platform, outlining which inventory items are excluded under the
''discontinued'' category. Distributor, perceiving this as informal and insufficient,
initially claims non-compliance, arguing that the communication does not meet the
standard of ''written notice.'' Nonetheless, Distributor proceeds to resell the
qualified inventory at the specified price, less any prior credits or allowances.
Separately, Distributor also raises concerns about Tripath''s refusal to purchase
some demonstration Products, despite these being excluded under the clause. Additionally,
Tripath exercises its option to apply a 10% restocking charge to some of the inventory,
leading to further friction. The situation appears questionable due to the informal
communication channel used by Tripath and the multiple deductions applied during
the transaction, giving the impression of non-compliance or bad faith actions. However,
all actions taken by Tripath strictly adhere to the letter of the agreement.'

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.

  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
